_source line 401 · 2026-05-21T04:20:58.035Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01DbNrRDocxMpgX9B1gRxyoG`</summary>

```text
1	
2	Synthetic data has become integral to language model training.
3	Instruction tuning, knowledge distillation, and data augmentation all routinely use model-generated text, and as synthetic corpora are recycled into training pipelines, the process often becomes recursive: each generation of models is fine-tuned on outputs from the previous one.
4	Recent work demonstrates that such recursive synthetic training triggers \textit{model collapse}, a progressive erosion of distributional tails and downstream capability~\citep{shumailov2024collapse, dohmatob2024tails, alemohammad2024mad}.
5	The problem is especially pressing as web-scraped corpora increasingly contain model-generated content, a trend that introduces implicit recursive loops even without deliberate data reuse.
6	
7	While model collapse is now well-characterized through theoretical analysis~\citep{dohmatob2024tails} and empirical studies~\citep{shumailov2024collapse, alemohammad2024mad}, its effect on the geometric structure of learned representations remains poorly understood.
8	Characterizing how the embedding space contracts during recursive training could yield computationally cheap collapse indicators from internal model states alone, without text generation or benchmark evaluation.
9	
10	Current collapse detection depends on downstream metrics such as perplexity and benchmark accuracy, or on analysis of generated text through lexical diversity measures.
11	Both approaches require either costly inference or external evaluation infrastructure at every generation.
12	SIGMA~\citep{gu2026sigma} introduced spectral monitoring through embedding covariance analysis and showed that spectral indicators can track collapse, but validated only on OPT-125M over short generation horizons.
13	Four gaps remain open.
14	First, no work has systematically characterized spectral dynamics beyond a few generations at 410M+ parameters.
15	Second, the temporal relationship between spectral indicators and downstream degradation has not been examined.
16	Third, no study has connected spectral indicators to closed-loop collapse prevention through the lens of early warning signals (EWS)~\citep{scheffer2009ews}.
17	Fourth, the relationship between real-data mixing ratios and spectral collapse has not been mapped: it is unknown how much real data suffices to prevent spectral contraction, or whether a sharp phase boundary exists.
18	
19	We address these gaps by tracking spectral indicators of the embedding Gram matrix across 10 recursive generations of Pythia-410M-deduped~\citep{biderman2023pythia}, with pilot validation on Pythia-1B-deduped.
20	Our analysis reveals a reproducible three-phase pattern (Figure~\ref{fig:spectral_trajectory}): spectral decompression (Gen~0--2), where effective rank ($r_{\text{eff}}$) and spectral log-volume (SLV) rise while downstream performance already declines; collapse onset (Gen~2--4), where spectral indicators reverse; and accelerating collapse (Gen~4--10), where contraction accelerates.
21	Among these indicators, SLV is particularly suited for automated monitoring: it requires only a forward pass on held-out data (no text generation), captures distributed volume changes across all spectral directions, and declines monotonically after its peak.
22	We further design a proof-of-concept proportional controller and discover a signal attenuation paradox: moderate real-data mixing prevents the spectral contraction that would trigger the controller, so reactive spectral feedback becomes ineffective precisely when collapse prevention succeeds.
23	
24	Our contributions are:
25	\begin{itemize}
26	\item \textbf{C1:} The first multi-seed spectral-downstream characterization at 410M+ scale, establishing a reproducible three-phase collapse pattern (decompression, onset, acceleration) across 10 generations (\S\ref{subsec:phase0}, Table~\ref{tab:phase0}).
27	\item \textbf{C2:} SLV as a computationally cheap collapse monitor with monotonic post-peak decline and a HellaSwag-spectral asymmetry: downstream degradation begins during the spectral decompression phase, demonstrating that spectral expansion does not indicate model health (\S\ref{subsec:phase0}).
28	\item \textbf{C3:} A signal attenuation paradox: moderate real-data mixing suppresses the spectral signal reactive controllers depend on, a structural limitation formalized via $r_{\text{crit}}$ (\S\ref{subsec:controller}, \S\ref{subsec:phase1}).
29	\item \textbf{C4:} A spectral collapse phase diagram that maps the boundary between collapse and stability across multiple mixing ratios, with empirical localization of $r_{\text{crit}}$ robust to the EMA smoothing parameter and random seed (\S\ref{subsec:phase_diagram}, \S\ref{subsec:phase1}, \S\ref{subsec:phase_diagram_exp}).
30	\end{itemize}
31	
```

```text
1	\subsection{Spectral Metrics}
2	\label{subsec:spectral_metrics}
3	
4	Given a language model at generation $t$, we extract last-layer embeddings for $n{=}1{,}000$ held-out samples to form $\mathbf{E} \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times d}$ and compute the Gram matrix $\mathbf{G} = \mathbf{E}^\top \mathbf{E}$.
5	Let $\sigma_1 \geq \sigma_2 \geq \cdots \geq \sigma_d$ denote the singular values of $\mathbf{E}$.
6	We track three complementary spectral indicators:
7	
8	\paragraph{Spectral Log-Volume (SLV).}
9	$\text{SLV} = \sum_i \log \sigma_i = \frac{1}{2} \log \det \mathbf{G}$.
10	This quantity measures the total volume spanned by the embedding cloud in log-space.
11	Because it sums over all singular values, SLV is sensitive to contraction along any spectral direction, not only the dominant ones.
12	
13	\paragraph{Effective Rank.}
14	$r_{\text{eff}} = \exp(H(\mathbf{p}))$, where $p_i = \sigma_i / \sum_j \sigma_j$ and $H$ is the Shannon entropy, measuring how many dimensions carry substantial variance (uniform $\to d$, concentrated $\to 1$).
15	
16	\paragraph{Leading Singular Value.}
17	$\sigma_1$ tracks the dominant spectral direction; rising $\sigma_1$ under falling $r_{\text{eff}}$ signals representational collapse into a low-dimensional subspace.
18	
19	\subsection{Adaptive Mixing Controller}
20	\label{subsec:controller}
21	
22	We propose a closed-loop controller that adjusts the real-data mixing ratio $r_t$ at each generation based on the rate of spectral change.
23	The update rule is:
24	\begin{equation}
25	\label{eq:controller}
26	r_{t+1} = \operatorname{clip}\!\bigl(\tfrac{B_{\text{rem}}}{T - t} + k \cdot \max(0,\,\Delta_t - \tau),\; r_{\min},\, r_{\max}\bigr)
27	\end{equation}
28	where $B_{\text{rem}}$ is the remaining real-data budget (fraction of total budget not yet consumed), $T$ is the total number of generations, $\tau{=}0.002$ is a sensitivity threshold, and $k{=}10$ is the controller gain.
29	The signal $\Delta_t$ is the baseline-normalized SLV drop: $\Delta_t = (\widetilde{S}_{t-1} - \widetilde{S}_t) / |\widetilde{S}_0|$, where $\widetilde{S}_t$ is the exponential moving average (EMA)-smoothed SLV ($\alpha{=}0.5$) and $\widetilde{S}_0$ is initialized from the pretrained model (Gen~0, SLV${=}2366.68$).
30	The signed form ensures that spectral decompression ($\Delta_t < 0$) does not trigger the controller; only contraction activates the $\max(0, \cdot)$ gate in Eq.~\ref{eq:controller}.
31	The output is clipped to $[r_{\min}, r_{\max}] = [0.20, 0.80]$ and subject to the global constraint $\sum_t r_t \leq B_{\text{total}}$.
32	
33	The first term distributes the remaining budget uniformly across future generations; the model degrades gracefully even without spectral signal.
34	The second term injects additional real data when SLV contraction exceeds the threshold $\tau$, proportional to the severity of the spectral drop.
35	This design requires no lookahead and operates with a single hyperparameter choice ($k$) beyond the budget constraint.
36	
37	\paragraph{Controller Silence Condition.}
38	Define $r_{\text{crit}}$ as the minimum constant mixing ratio such that the EMA-smoothed SLV is non-decreasing from the second generation onward: $\widetilde{S}_t \geq \widetilde{S}_{t-1}$ for all $t \in \{2, \ldots, T\}$ (excluding the initial fine-tuning transient at $t{=}1$). For conditions with multiple seeds, the condition is evaluated on the cross-seed mean trajectory.
39	When a constant ratio $r \geq r_{\text{crit}}$ is applied, $\Delta_t \leq 0 < \tau$ for all $t \geq 2$ and any $\tau > 0$, so the controller boost is identically zero.
40	This $\tau$-independence means the paradox cannot be resolved by lowering the sensitivity threshold; the same argument extends to cumulative variants.
41	Our experiments (\S\ref{subsec:phase1}) empirically bound $0.25 < r_{\text{crit}} \leq 0.3$ over Gen~2--10 for Pythia-410M on OpenWebText.
42	
43	\paragraph{Second-Order Extension.}
44	\label{subsec:second_order}
45	A natural extension monitors the rate of change of the controller signal itself:
46	\begin{equation}
47	\label{eq:second_order}
48	\Delta^2_t = \widetilde{\Delta}_{t-1} - \widetilde{\Delta}_t
49	\end{equation}
50	where $\widetilde{\Delta}_t$ is the EMA-smoothed first-order signal ($\alpha_2{=}0.3$).
51	A sustained negative $\Delta^2_t$ indicates decelerating SLV growth, a potential precursor to stagnation even when SLV is still rising.
52	We evaluate this indicator in \S\ref{subsec:phase_diagram_exp}.
53	
54	\subsection{Collapse Phase Diagram}
55	\label{subsec:phase_diagram}
56	
57	We define the \emph{collapse phase diagram} as the mapping from constant mixing ratio $r$ to the SLV trajectory over $T$ generations.
58	For each $r$, we classify the outcome: \emph{collapse} if SLV declines monotonically after the decompression phase (Gen~2 onward), \emph{stable} if SLV is non-decreasing, and \emph{transitional} if SLV shows non-monotonic behavior after Gen~2 (both increases and decreases within the observation window).
59	The critical mixing ratio $r_{\text{crit}}$ (\S\ref{subsec:controller}) is the boundary between collapse and stability in this diagram.
60	We estimate $r_{\text{crit}}$ by evaluating multiple constant ratios and identifying the lowest $r$ for which $\widetilde{S}_t \geq \widetilde{S}_{t-1}$ holds for all $t \in \{2, \ldots, T\}$.
61

```text
1	
2	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
3	\label{subsec:setup}
4	
5	We use Pythia-410M-deduped \citep{biderman2023pythia} as our base model and fine-tune on a 50K-subset of OpenWebText.
6	Starting from the pretrained checkpoint (generation~0), each subsequent generation fine-tunes on synthetic text produced by the previous generation, for a total of 10 recursive generations.
7	At each generation we compute the spectral indicators from \S\ref{subsec:spectral_metrics} on the Gram matrix of 1K held-out last-layer embeddings, paired with three downstream metrics: perplexity, HellaSwag accuracy (0-shot), and distinct-4.
8	These three metrics (language modeling capability, natural language understanding, and generation diversity) cover the primary evaluation dimensions accessible to a 410M-parameter model.
9	
10	For the mixing comparison (\S\ref{subsec:phase1}), we evaluate three strategies sharing the same total real-data budget:
11	(1)~\textbf{pure collapse} ($r_t{=}0$),
12	(2)~\textbf{fixed mix} ($r_t{=}0.50$), and
13	(3)~\textbf{adaptive} ($k{=}10$, Eq.~\ref{eq:controller}).
14	
15	\subsection{Spectral Collapse Characterization}
16	\label{subsec:phase0}
17	
18	Table~\ref{tab:phase0} reports spectral and downstream metrics across 10 recursive generations, which reveal a three-phase structure.
19	
20	\begin{table}[t]
21	\centering
22	\small
23	\begin{tabular}{@{}rrrrrrc@{}}
24	\toprule
25	Gen & $r_{\text{eff}}$ & SLV & PPL & HeSw & D-4 & $\sigma_1$ \\
26	\midrule
27	0  & 417.55 & 2366.68 &  9.55 & .413 & .741 & 1942.8 \\
28	2  & \textbf{496.34} & \textbf{2468.28} & 13.57 & .378 & .510 &  715.1 \\
29	5  & 479.64 & 2367.65 & 24.60 & .341 & .440 &  747.3 \\
30	7  & 464.19 & 2297.87 & 38.17 & .319 & .419 &  790.7 \\
31	10 & 440.62 & 2194.86 & 73.64 & .293 & .410 &  855.9 \\
32	\bottomrule
33	\end{tabular}
34	\caption{Spectral and downstream metrics at selected generations (Pythia-410M, mean of seeds 42 and 43; sample s.d.\ at Gen~10: $r_{\text{eff}}$ $\pm$2.6, SLV $\pm$22.5, PPL $\pm$4.4, HeSw $\pm$.004). Bold marks the spectral peak.}
35	\label{tab:phase0}
36	\end{table}
37	
38	\begin{figure}[t]
39	\centering
40	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/spectral_trajectory.pdf}
41	\caption{SLV across 10 recursive generations of Pythia-410M under pure-synthetic training (solid) and 50\% real-data mixing (dashed). Three phases are visible in the pure-synthetic trajectory: decompression (Gen~0--2, SLV peaks at 2468), onset (Gen~2--4), and acceleration (Gen~4--10, SLV falls 11\% from peak to 2195). Under 50\% mixing, SLV rises monotonically (except a negligible Gen~1 dip) to 2572 at Gen~10, 8.7\% above baseline and 104 points above the pure-collapse peak.}
42	\label{fig:spectral_trajectory}
43	\end{figure}
44	
45	\paragraph{Phase I: Spectral Decompression (Gen~0--2).}
46	We identify phase boundaries at inflection points where $r_{\text{eff}}$ and SLV transition from rising to declining.
47	In the first two generations, $r_{\text{eff}}$ rises from 417.6 to 496.3 (+18.9\%) and SLV increases from 2366.7 to 2468.3 (+4.3\%), while $\sigma_1$ drops sharply from 1942.8 to 715.1 as spectral mass redistributes from the dominant direction to smaller components.
48	The consistency of the peak position (Gen~2 across both seeds) and its coincidence with the qualitative reversal of $\sigma_1$ dynamics (from declining to rising) suggest that the phase boundary reflects a structural transition rather than a statistical artifact.
49	Crucially, this decompression does not indicate improved model quality: HellaSwag drops from 0.413 to 0.378 ($-$8.5\%) and distinct-4 falls from 0.741 to 0.510 ($-$31.2\%) during the same interval.
50	HellaSwag already declines 5.6\% from Gen~0 to Gen~1, precisely when spectral indicators are still rising; this asymmetry suggests that decompression captures distributional broadening rather than preservation of task-supporting representations.
51	A monitoring system relying solely on spectral metrics during Phase~I would incorrectly conclude the model remains healthy; joint spectral-downstream tracking~\citep{gu2026sigma} is essential to detect early collapse.
52	The value of SLV lies not in temporal precedence over downstream metrics, but in requiring no text generation or external benchmark evaluation; it supports continuous monitoring at training time.
53	
54	\paragraph{Phase II: Collapse Onset (Gen~2--4).}
55	Both $r_{\text{eff}}$ and SLV reverse after Gen~2 ($r_{\text{eff}}$: 496.3$\to$493.7, SLV: 2468$\to$2439), and this reversal marks the transition from decompression to collapse while perplexity continues its monotonic rise.
56	
57	\paragraph{Phase III: Accelerating Collapse (Gen~4--10).}
58	Spectral contraction accelerates: the mean per-generation $r_{\text{eff}}$ decline in Phase~III is $1.67 \pm 0.18$\%/gen (mean $\pm$ s.d.\ across two seeds).
59	By Gen~10, $r_{\text{eff}}$ falls to 440.6 ($-$11.2\% from peak), SLV drops to 2194.9 ($-$7.3\% from baseline), perplexity reaches 73.6 ($+$671\%), and $\sigma_1$ reverses its earlier decline, reaching 855.9 at Gen~10, a sign of renewed spectral concentration (Figure~\ref{fig:spectral_trajectory}).
60	
61	\paragraph{Cross-Scale Pilot.}
62	Repeating the protocol on Pythia-1B (Gen~0--3) confirms the same decompression-onset structure ($r_{\text{eff}}$ peak at Gen~2, SLV peak one generation earlier). SLV's low cost and monotonic post-peak decline make it well-suited as an automated collapse monitor.
63	In our setup, SLV computation (1{,}000-sample forward pass plus SVD of the $d {\times} d$ Gram matrix) takes ${<}\,1$~second on a single GPU, whereas HellaSwag 0-shot evaluation requires ${\sim}70$~minutes end-to-end under identical hardware.
64	
65	\subsection{Adaptive Mixing Comparison}
66	\label{subsec:phase1}
67	
68	Under pure synthetic training (\S\ref{subsec:phase0}), SLV peaks at Gen~2 (2468) and declines monotonically thereafter.
69	With 50\% real-data mixing, SLV rises over nine of ten generations, reaching 2572 at Gen~10 (104 points above the pure-collapse peak); a negligible dip at Gen~1 ($\Delta_1 \approx 0.0003$, well below $\tau$) reflects the initial fine-tuning transient.
70	
71	The adaptive controller ($k{=}10$) remains silent through all ten generations: $\Delta_t$ is non-positive from Gen~2 onward, and the sole positive value ($\Delta_1 \approx 0.0003$) falls $6\times$ below $\tau{=}0.002$.
72	Since $\Delta_t \leq 0$ for all $t \geq 2$, the controller stays silent for any finite $k$ and any $\tau > 0$ (\S\ref{subsec:controller}); larger gain values do not escape the paradox.
73	The silence is non-trivial because the controller monitors the \emph{rate} of spectral change, not the level; even moderate mixing that does not prevent downstream degradation (PPL $+$45\%, D-4 $-$30\% at $r{=}0.2$) suffices to suppress the rate signal below $\tau$.
74	Since pure collapse ($r{=}0$) produces SLV decline from Gen~3 while fixed mix ($r{=}0.5$) yields sustained growth from Gen~2, and $r{=}0.2$ falls below $r_{\text{crit}}$ (\S\ref{subsec:phase_diagram_exp}), we bound $0.2 < r_{\text{crit}} \leq 0.5$.
75	
76	Despite sustained spectral expansion, downstream metrics degrade steadily: perplexity increases 58\% ($9.55 \to 15.10$) and distinct-4 falls 87\% ($0.741 \to 0.095$) over ten generations, the lowest D-4 of any condition, below even pure collapse ($r{=}0$: 0.410).
77	This reinforces the asymmetry from \S\ref{subsec:phase0}: spectral health under mixed training does not guarantee model health, and the signal attenuation paradox renders per-generation reactive controllers ineffective precisely when collapse prevention succeeds.
78	
79	\subsection{Alternative EWS Comparison}
80	\label{subsec:alt_ews}
81	
82	To assess whether the spectral patterns in \S\ref{subsec:phase0} reflect a broader embedding-level geometric phenomenon rather than an artifact of a single metric, we compare SLV against six alternative collapse indicators from three signal sources: embedding geometry (RankMe, isotropy), attention weights (stable rank, spectral norm), and output distributions (token entropy, perplexity growth rate).
83	Token entropy is the Shannon entropy of the argmax logit distribution over a fixed evaluation set and requires no text generation.
84	
85	Table~\ref{tab:ews_comparison} summarizes the comparison under pure-synthetic training ($r{=}0$).
86	Two indicators share SLV's decompression-then-collapse trajectory: RankMe~\citep{rankme2023}, the exponential of the normalized singular-value entropy, peaks at Gen~2 and declines monotonically thereafter, though with 2.5$\times$ lower signal-to-noise ratio (4.7\% relative change vs.\ 11.8\% for SLV).
87	Weight-level and output-level indicators (stable rank, spectral norm, token entropy) change monotonically from Gen~0 with no decompression phase; they cannot distinguish collapse onset from the initial fine-tuning transient.
88	Isotropy is the least reliable indicator: random sampling without a fixed seed introduces approximately 5\% variance, and it exhibits six monotonicity violations across ten generations, with direction reversals at Gen~4+ that produce a misleading recovery signal.
89	
90	\begin{table}[t]
91	\centering
92	\small
93	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
94	\begin{tabular}{@{}llcrrc@{}}
95	\toprule
96	Indicator & Source & Peak & Mono? & Rel $\Delta$ & Rev.? \\
97	\midrule
98	SLV         & emb-Gram  & Gen 2 & \checkmark & 11.8\% & \checkmark \\
99	RankMe      & emb-SVD   & Gen 2 & \checkmark &  4.7\% & \checkmark \\
100	Token ent.  & logit     & ---   & \checkmark &  9.6\% & \checkmark \\
101	Stable rank & attn wt.  & ---   & \checkmark & 14.0\% & \checkmark \\
102	Spec.\ norm & attn wt.  & ---   & \checkmark &  8.0\% & \checkmark \\
103	Isotropy    & emb       & ---   & $\times$\,(6) & 75\%   & \checkmark \\
104	PPL growth  & ppl       & ---   & $\sim$\,(1) &  ---   & \checkmark \\
105	\bottomrule
106	\end{tabular}
107	\caption{Alternative early warning indicators under pure-synthetic training ($r{=}0$, Pythia-410M, 10 gen). \textbf{Peak}: generation at which the indicator reverses (--- = monotonic from Gen~0). \textbf{Mono?}: post-peak monotonicity (\checkmark\ = yes; number = violations). \textbf{Rel $\Delta$}: total relative change Gen~0--10. \textbf{Rev.?}: direction reversal under $r{=}0.5$ mixing (\checkmark\ = reversed).}
108	\label{tab:ews_comparison}
109	\end{table}
110	
111	The final column of Table~\ref{tab:ews_comparison} reveals that all seven indicators reverse direction under 50\% real-data mixing.
112	This universality confirms that the signal attenuation paradox (\S\ref{subsec:controller}) is not specific to SLV: any per-generation reactive controller based on these indicators will fall silent when mixing exceeds $r_{\text{crit}}$.
113	Among embedding-level metrics, SLV offers the highest signal-to-noise ratio (2.5$\times$ that of RankMe) and, together with RankMe, is the only indicator that exhibits a decompression baseline to distinguish collapse onset from training transients.
114	
115	\subsection{Collapse Phase Diagram}
116	\label{subsec:phase_diagram_exp}
117	
118	We now map SLV trajectories across mixing ratios to construct the collapse phase diagram defined in \S\ref{subsec:phase_diagram}.
119	Figure~\ref{fig:phase_diagram} plots SLV across 10 recursive generations for five mixing ratios; three qualitatively distinct trajectory types emerge:
120	
121	\begin{figure}[t]
122	\centering
123	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/phase_diagram.pdf}
124	\caption{SLV trajectories across mixing ratios. Type~I ($r{=}0$): decompression then monotonic collapse. Type~II ($r{=}0.2$, $r{=}0.25$): transient dip followed by recovery. Type~III ($r{=}0.3$, $r{=}0.5$): monotonic expansion. The shaded band marks the critical threshold $r_{\text{crit}} \in (0.25, 0.3]$. The dashed horizontal line indicates the Gen~0 baseline.}
125	\label{fig:phase_diagram}
126	\end{figure}
127	
128	\paragraph{Type~I: Pure Collapse ($r{=}0$).}
129	SLV exhibits large decompression ($+$97 points at Gen~1, peaking at Gen~2) followed by accelerating contraction (\S\ref{subsec:phase0}).
130	This trajectory defines the baseline collapse regime.
131	
132	\paragraph{Type~II: Near-critical Mixing ($r{=}0.2$).}
133	Twenty percent real data suppresses the SLV decompression phase: SLV rises only 0.78 points from Gen~0 to Gen~1 (vs.\ $+$98 under $r{=}0$), dips at Gen~2 ($2367.5 \to 2358.3$, $-9.2$), then recovers from Gen~3 with per-generation gains accelerating from $+4.2$ (Gen~3) to $+30.9$ (Gen~6) before moderating through Gen~10, reaching 2479.7 at Gen~10 ($+4.8\%$ above baseline).
134	Because $\widetilde{S}_2 = 2362.7 < \widetilde{S}_1 = 2367.1$ (EMA-smoothed SLV; \S\ref{subsec:controller}), the non-decreasing condition is violated and $r{=}0.2$ formally lies below $r_{\text{crit}}$.
135	The trajectory is consistent with the transitional regime (\S\ref{subsec:phase_diagram}): initial contraction followed by sustained recovery over the full 10-generation horizon.
136	The smoothed signal never exceeds the controller threshold ($\max \Delta_t = 0.0019$ at Gen~2, below $\tau = 0.002$), so as little as 20\% real data silences the controller entirely.
137	The effective rank still rises at Gen~2 ($476.7 \to 481.6$) while the EMA-smoothed SLV registers a net decline ($\widetilde{S}_2 < \widetilde{S}_1$), evidence that smoothed SLV detects spectral contraction earlier at this mixing ratio.
138	
139	At $r{=}0.25$, the full 10-generation trajectory follows the same Type~II pattern: SLV dips from Gen~0 to Gen~1 ($2366.7 \to 2362.1$, $-4.6$), reaches a minimum at Gen~2 ($2363.1$), then rises monotonically through Gen~10 ($2485.0$, $+5.0\%$ above baseline).
140	The EMA-smoothed sequence confirms the sub-critical classification: $\widetilde{S}_1 = 2364.4$, $\widetilde{S}_2 = 2363.8$, satisfying $\widetilde{S}_2 < \widetilde{S}_1$ and violating the non-decreasing condition.
141	Controller silence is more pronounced than at $r{=}0.2$: $\max \Delta_t = 0.00028 < \tau = 0.002$, a $6.7\times$ weaker signal than the $r{=}0.2$ maximum ($0.0019$), consistent with further signal attenuation as the real-data fraction increases.
142	The amplitude of the EMA violation at $r{=}0.25$ varies substantially across seeds ($\widetilde{S}_2 - \widetilde{S}_1 = -0.6$ vs.\ $-1.7$), consistent with the elevated fluctuations expected near a phase boundary, while trajectories at $r{=}0$ and $r \geq 0.3$ show low cross-seed variance ($< 0.5\%$).
143	
144	\paragraph{Type~III: Super-critical Mixing ($r{=}0.5$).}
145	SLV rises monotonically from Gen~2 onward, reaching 2572 at Gen~10 ($+$8.7\% above baseline), consistent with $r{=}0.5 \geq r_{\text{crit}}$ (\S\ref{subsec:phase1}).
146	Per-generation gains remain positive through all post-transient generations ($\Delta_t \in [+11, +35]$ for $t \geq 2$; mean $+23.0$), sustaining expansion with no reversal toward the contraction trajectory of Type~I within the observed horizon.
147	
148	These three types partition the mixing-ratio axis into collapse ($r < r_{\text{crit}}$), transitional, and stable ($r \geq r_{\text{crit}}$) zones.
149	Type classification is based solely on spectral trajectory shape; downstream metrics such as D-4 may diverge, as demonstrated by the non-monotonic D-4 pattern across mixing ratios (Table~\ref{tab:phase_summary}).
150	At $r{=}0.3$, SLV expands monotonically from Gen~2 to 2509.7 at Gen~10 ($+$6.0\% above baseline), with sustained positive growth across all eight post-transient generations.
151	This confirms $r{=}0.3 \geq r_{\text{crit}}$ over the full 10-generation horizon, tightening the bound to $0.25 < r_{\text{crit}} \leq 0.3$.
152	Under a second seed, the individual EMA-smoothed sequence shows a transient violation of 0.83~points at Gen~2; however, the cross-seed mean trajectory satisfies the non-decreasing condition at all generations, consistent with the Type~III classification.
153	The critical threshold is robust to the smoothing parameter: recomputing the EMA-smoothed SLV for $\alpha \in \{0.3, 0.5, 0.7\}$ yields $r_{\text{crit}} \in (0.25, 0.3]$ in all cases, with the non-decreasing condition violated at Gen~2 for $r \leq 0.25$ and satisfied on the cross-seed mean for $r \geq 0.3$ regardless of $\alpha$.
154	
155	The largest single-generation SLV drop in the first two generations shrinks with $r$: $-9.2$ at $r{=}0.2$ (Gen~1$\to$2), $-4.6$ at $r{=}0.25$ (Gen~0$\to$1), and $-0.6$ at $r{=}0.3$ (Gen~0$\to$1), consistent with a continuous crossover rather than a sharp boundary.
156	The Type~II trajectory clarifies what $r_{\text{crit}}$ marks in practice: not a boundary between collapse and stability in final outcome, but a transition in trajectory shape.
157	Below $r_{\text{crit}}$, real-data mixing fails to suppress the initial SLV contraction transient but sustains spectral expansion through Gen~10 once the transient passes.
158	\paragraph{Output Diversity Divergence.}
159	At $r{=}0.2$, D-4 exhibits a V-shaped recovery (0.438 at Gen~5 $\to$ 0.520 at Gen~10), but this pattern does not generalize: at $r{=}0.25$, D-4 declines monotonically to 0.357 despite comparable SLV recovery, indicating that spectral expansion does not guarantee diversity restoration.
160	More broadly, D-4 exhibits a non-monotonic relationship with $r$: it peaks at $r{=}0.2$ (.520), exceeds the pure-collapse baseline (.410) only at this ratio, and drops to .095 at $r{=}0.5$, the lowest across all conditions (Table~\ref{tab:phase_summary}).
161	This pattern is consistent with increasing real-data anchoring: at high $r$, the model converges toward the fixed real-data distribution, producing coherent but stylistically homogeneous text.
162	This divergence between spectral health (monotonically improving with $r$) and n-gram diversity underscores that SLV and D-4 capture orthogonal aspects of model behavior.
163	D-4 exhibits substantially higher cross-seed variance than spectral metrics at $r{=}0.3$ (s.d.\ $= 0.156$ vs.\ SLV s.d.\ $= 2.0$; Table~\ref{tab:phase_summary}), suggesting that n-gram diversity reflects stochastic generation choices rather than the deterministic geometric structure captured by SLV.
164	
165	Table~\ref{tab:phase_summary} consolidates the final-generation metrics across all five mixing ratios.
166	
167	\begin{table}[t]
168	\centering
169	\small
170	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
171	\begin{tabular}{@{}clrrrrrc@{}}
172	\toprule
173	$r$ & Type & SLV & $r_{\text{eff}}$ & PPL & D-4 & $\max\Delta_t$ & Gen. \\
174	\midrule
175	0    & I   & 2194.9 & 440.6 & 73.64 & .410 & ---   & 10 \\
176	0.20 & II  & 2479.7 & 511.3 & 13.83 & .520 & .0019 & 10 \\
177	0.25 & II  & 2485.0 & 512.5 & 13.29 & .357 & .0003 & 10 \\
178	0.30 & III & 2509.7 & 521.2 & 13.34 & .232 & 0     & 10 \\
179	0.50 & III & 2572.2 & 553.8 & 15.10 & .095 & 0     & 10 \\
180	\bottomrule
181	\end{tabular}
182	\caption{Final-generation spectral and downstream metrics across mixing ratios (all runs to Gen~10). $\max\Delta_t$ computed for $t \geq 2$, excluding the initial fine-tuning transient. Type classification follows \S\ref{subsec:phase_diagram}. $r{=}0$ and $r{=}0.3$ report the mean of two seeds; $r{=}0.2$, $r{=}0.25$, and $r{=}0.5$ report a single seed. D-4 at $r{=}0.3$ exhibits high cross-seed variance (s.d.\ $= 0.156$).}
183	\label{tab:phase_summary}
184	\end{table}
185	
186	\paragraph{Second-order indicator.}
187	We evaluate $\Delta^2_t$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:second_order}, $\alpha_2{=}0.3$) across the four mixing ratios with multi-generation data.
188	$\Delta^2_t$ turns negative only in the final 1--3 generations for all ratios: Gen~10 at $r{=}0.25$ ($-2.6{\times}10^{-4}$), Gen~9--10 at $r{=}0.3$ ($-1.1$ to $-1.4{\times}10^{-4}$), and Gen~8--10 at $r{=}0.5$ ($-1.1$ to $-6.5{\times}10^{-4}$).
189	Because this late-stage deceleration appears universally, including in Type~III trajectories with no collapse risk, it does not discriminate between sub-critical and super-critical regimes within the 10-generation horizon observed here, consistent with the hedged assessment in \S\ref{subsec:controller}.
190	We chose the simplest second-order extension; cumulative or variance-based indicators~\citep{scheffer2009ews} merit investigation but require longer time series than the 10-generation horizon available here, as their statistical power scales with sample size.
191	Finer resolution between 0.25 and 0.3 would narrow the bound but would not change the qualitative finding or the practical recommendation to set $r$ above 0.3.
192	A level-based trigger keyed to baseline SLV could detect the transient dip in Type~II trajectories, but would not activate under Type~III where SLV exceeds baseline at all generations.
193

      "content": "\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{subsec:setup}\n\nWe use Pythia-410M-deduped \\citep{biderman2023pythia} as our base model and fine-tune on a 50K-subset of OpenWebText.\nStarting from the pretrained checkpoint (generation~0), each subsequent generation fine-tunes on synthetic text produced by the previous generation, for a total of 10 recursive generations.\nAt each generation we compute the spectral indicators from \\S\\ref{subsec:spectral_metrics} on the Gram matrix of 1K held-out last-layer embeddings, paired with three downstream metrics: perplexity, HellaSwag accuracy (0-shot), and distinct-4.\nThese three metrics (language modeling capability, natural language understanding, and generation diversity) cover the primary evaluation dimensions accessible to a 410M-parameter model.\n\nFor the mixing comparison (\\S\\ref{subsec:phase1}), we evaluate three strategies sharing the same total real-data budget:\n(1)~\\textbf{pure collapse} ($r_t{=}0$),\n(2)~\\textbf{fixed mix} ($r_t{=}0.50$), and\n(3)~\\textbf{adaptive} ($k{=}10$, Eq.~\\ref{eq:controller}).\n\n\\subsection{Spectral Collapse Characterization}\n\\label{subsec:phase0}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:phase0} reports spectral and downstream metrics across 10 recursive generations, which reveal a three-phase structure.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}rrrrrrc@{}}\n\\toprule\nGen & $r_{\\text{eff}}$ & SLV & PPL & HeSw & D-4 & $\\sigma_1$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0  & 417.55 & 2366.68 &  9.55 & .413 & .741 & 1942.8 \\\\\n2  & \\textbf{496.34} & \\textbf{2468.28} & 13.57 & .378 & .510 &  715.1 \\\\\n5  & 479.64 & 2367.65 & 24.60 & .341 & .440 &  747.3 \\\\\n7  & 464.19 & 2297.87 & 38.17 & .319 & .419 &  790.7 \\\\\n10 & 440.62 & 2194.86 & 73.64 & .293 & .410 &  855.9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Spectral and downstream metrics at selected generations (Pythia-410M, mean of seeds 42 and 43; sample s.d.\\ at Gen~10: $r_{\\text{eff}}$ $\\pm$2.6, SLV $\\pm$22.5, PPL $\\pm$4.4, HeSw $\\pm$.004). Bold marks the spectral peak.}\n\\label{tab:phase0}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/spectral_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{SLV across 10 recursive generations of Pythia-410M under pure-synthetic training (solid) and 50\\% real-data mixing (dashed). Three phases are visible in the pure-synthetic trajectory: decompression (Gen~0--2, SLV peaks at 2468), onset (Gen~2--4), and acceleration (Gen~4--10, SLV falls 11\\% from peak to 2195). Under 50\\% mixing, SLV rises monotonically (except a negligible Gen~1 dip) to 2572 at Gen~10, 8.7\\% above baseline and 104 points above the pure-collapse peak.}\n\\label{fig:spectral_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Phase I: Spectral Decompression (Gen~0--2).}\nWe identify phase boundaries at inflection points where $r_{\\text{eff}}$ and SLV transition from rising to declining.\nIn the first two generations, $r_{\\text{eff}}$ rises from 417.6 to 496.3 (+18.9\\%) and SLV increases from 2366.7 to 2468.3 (+4.3\\%), while $\\sigma_1$ drops sharply from 1942.8 to 715.1 as spectral mass redistributes from the dominant direction to smaller components.\nThe consistency of the peak position (Gen~2 across both seeds) and its coincidence with the qualitative reversal of $\\sigma_1$ dynamics (from declining to rising) suggest that the phase boundary reflects a structural transition rather than a statistical artifact.\nCrucially, this decompression does not indicate improved model quality: HellaSwag drops from 0.413 to 0.378 ($-$8.5\\%) and distinct-4 falls from 0.741 to 0.510 ($-$31.2\\%) during the same interval.\nHellaSwag already declines 5.6\\% from Gen~0 to Gen~1, precisely when spectral indicators are still rising; this asymmetry suggests that decompression captures distributional broadening rather than preservation of task-supporting representations.\nA monitoring system relying solely on spectral metrics during Phase~I would incorrectly conclude the model remains healthy; joint spectral-downstream tracking~\\citep{gu2026sigma} is essential to detect early collapse.\nThe value of SLV lies not in temporal precedence over downstream metrics, but in requiring no text generation or external benchmark evaluation; it supports continuous monitoring at training time.\n\n\\paragraph{Phase II: Collapse Onset (Gen~2--4).}\nBoth $r_{\\text{eff}}$ and SLV reverse after Gen~2 ($r_{\\text{eff}}$: 496.3$\\to$493.7, SLV: 2468$\\to$2439), and this reversal marks the transition from decompression to collapse while perplexity continues its monotonic rise.\n\n\\paragraph{Phase III: Accelerating Collapse (Gen~4--10).}\nSpectral contraction accelerates: the mean per-generation $r_{\\text{eff}}$ decline in Phase~III is $1.67 \\pm 0.18$\\%/gen (mean $\\pm$ s.d.\\ across two seeds).\nBy Gen~10, $r_{\\text{eff}}$ falls to 440.6 ($-$11.2\\% from peak), SLV drops to 2194.9 ($-$7.3\\% from baseline), perplexity reaches 73.6 ($+$671\\%), and $\\sigma_1$ reverses its earlier decline, reaching 855.9 at Gen~10, a sign of renewed spectral concentration (Figure~\\ref{fig:spectral_trajectory}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-Scale Pilot.}\nRepeating the protocol on Pythia-1B (Gen~0--3) confirms the same decompression-onset structure ($r_{\\text{eff}}$ peak at Gen~2, SLV peak one generation earlier). SLV's low cost and monotonic post-peak decline make it well-suited as an automated collapse monitor.\nIn our setup, SLV computation (1{,}000-sample forward pass plus SVD of the $d {\\times} d$ Gram matrix) takes ${<}\\,1$~second on a single GPU, whereas HellaSwag 0-shot evaluation requires ${\\sim}70$~minutes end-to-end under identical hardware.\n\n\\subsection{Adaptive Mixing Comparison}\n\\label{subsec:phase1}\n\nUnder pure synthetic training (\\S\\ref{subsec:phase0}), SLV peaks at Gen~2 (2468) and declines monotonically thereafter.\nWith 50\\% real-data mixing, SLV rises over nine of ten generations, reaching 2572 at Gen~10 (104 points above the pure-collapse peak); a negligible dip at Gen~1 ($\\Delta_1 \\approx 0.0003$, well below $\\tau$) reflects the initial fine-tuning transient.\n\nThe adaptive controller ($k{=}10$) remains silent through all ten generations: $\\Delta_t$ is non-positive from Gen~2 onward, and the sole positive value ($\\Delta_1 \\approx 0.0003$) falls $6\\times$ below $\\tau{=}0.002$.\nSince $\\Delta_t \\leq 0$ for all $t \\geq 2$, the controller stays silent for any finite $k$ and any $\\tau > 0$ (\\S\\ref{subsec:controller}); larger gain values do not escape the paradox.\nThe silence is non-trivial because the controller monitors the \\emph{rate} of spectral change, not the level; even moderate mixing that does not prevent downstream degradation (PPL $+$45\\%, D-4 $-$30\\% at $r{=}0.2$) suffices to suppress the rate signal below $\\tau$.\nSince pure collapse ($r{=}0$) produces SLV decline from Gen~3 while fixed mix $r{=}0.3$, SLV expands monotonically from Gen~2 to 2509.7 at Gen~10 ($+$6.0\\% above baseline), with sustained positive growth across all eight post-transient generations.\nThis confirms $r{=}0.3 \\geq r_{\\text{crit}}$ over the full 10-generation horizon, tightening the bound to $0.25 < r_{\\text{crit}} \\leq 0.3$.\nUnder a second seed, the individual EMA-smoothed sequence shows a transient violation of 0.83~points at Gen~2; however, the cross-seed mean trajectory satisfies the non-decreasing condition at all generations, consistent with the Type~III classification.\nThe critical threshold is robust to the smoothing parameter: recomputing the EMA-smoothed SLV for $\\alpha \\in \\{0.3, 0.5, 0.7\\}$ yields $r_{\\text{crit}} \\in (0.25, 0.3]$ in all cases, with the non-decreasing condition violated at Gen~2 for $r \\leq 0.25$ and satisfied on the cross-seed mean for $r \\geq 0.3$ regardless of $\\alpha$.\n\nThe largest single-generation SLV drop in the first two generations shrinks with $r$: $-9.2$ at $r{=}0.2$ (Gen~1$\\to$2), $-4.6$ at $r{=}0.25$ (Gen~0$\\to$1), and $-0.6$ at $r{=}0.3$ (Gen~0$\\to$1), consistent with a continuous crossover rather than a sharp boundary.\nThe Type~II trajectory clarifies what $r_{\\text{crit}}$ marks in practice: not a boundary between collapse and stability in final outcome, but a transition in trajectory shape.\nBelow $r_{\\text{crit}}$, real-data mixing fails to suppress the initial SLV contraction transient but sustains spectral expansion through Gen~10 once the transient passes.\n\\paragraph{Output Diversity Divergence.}\nAt $r{=}0.2$, D-4 exhibits a V-shaped recovery (0.438 at Gen~5 $\\to$ 0.520 at Gen~10), but this pattern does not generalize: at $r{=}0.25$, D-4 declines monotonically to 0.357 despite comparable SLV recovery, indicating that spectral expansion does not guarantee diversity restoration.\nMore broadly, D-4 exhibits a non-monotonic relationship with $r$: it peaks at $r{=}0.2$ (.520), exceeds the pure-collapse baseline (.410) only at this ratio, and drops to .095 at $r{=}0.5$, the lowest across all conditions (Table~\\ref{tab:phase_summary}).\nThis pattern is consistent with increasing real-data anchoring: at high $r$, the model converges toward the fixed real-data distribution, producing coherent but stylistically homogeneous text.\nThis divergence between spectral health (monotonically improving with $r$) and n-gram diversity underscores that SLV and D-4 capture orthogonal aspects of model behavior.\nD-4 exhibits substantially higher cross-seed variance than spectral metrics at $r{=}0.3$ (s.d.\\ $= 0.156$ vs.\\ SLV s.d.\\ $= 2.0$; Table~\\ref{tab:phase_summary}), suggesting that n-gram diversity reflects stochastic generation choices rather than the deterministic geometric structure captured by SLV.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:phase_summary} consolidates the final-generation metrics across all five mixing ratios.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}clrrrrrc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$r$ & Type & SLV & $r_{\\text{eff}}$ & PPL & D-4 & $\\max\\Delta_t$ & Gen. \\\\\n\\midrule\n0    & I   & 2194.9 & 440.6 & 73.64 & .410 & ---   & 10 \\\\\n0.20 & II  & 2479.7 & 511.3 & 13.83 & .520 & .0019 & 10 \\\\\n0.25 & II  & 2485.0 & 512.5 & 13.29 & .357 & .0003 & 10 \\\\\n0.30 & III & 2509.7 & 521.2 & 13.34 & .232 & 0     & 10 \\\\\n0.50 & III & 2572.2 & 553.8 & 15.10 & .095 & 0     & 10 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Final-generation spectral and downstream metrics across mixing ratios (all runs to Gen~10). $\\max\\Delta_t$ computed for $t \\geq 2$, excluding the initial fine-tuning transient. Type classification follows \\S\\ref{subsec:phase_diagram}. $r{=}0$ and $r{=}0.3$ report the mean of two seeds; $r{=}0.2$, $r{=}0.25$, and $r{=}0.5$ report a single seed. D-4 at $r{=}0.3$ exhibits high cross-seed variance (s.d.\\ $= 0.156$).}\n\\label{tab:phase_summary}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Second-order indicator.}\nWe evaluate $\\Delta^2_t$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:second_order}, $\\alpha_2{=}0.3$) across the four mixing ratios with multi-generation data.\n$\\Delta^2_t$ turns negative only in the final 1--3 generations for all ratios: Gen~10 at $r{=}0.25$ ($-2.6{\\times}10^{-4}$), Gen~9--10 at $r{=}0.3$ ($-1.1$ to $-1.4{\\times}10^{-4}$), and Gen~8--10 at $r{=}0.5$ ($-1.1$ to $-6.5{\\times}10^{-4}$).\nBecause this late-stage deceleration appears universally, including in Type~III trajectories with no collapse risk, it does not discriminate between sub-critical and super-critical regimes within the 10-generation horizon observed here, consistent with the hedged assessment in \\S\\ref{subsec:controller}.\nWe chose the simplest second-order extension; cumulative or variance-based indicators~\\citep{scheffer2009ews} merit investigation but require longer time series than the 10-generation horizon available here, as their statistical power scales with sample size.\nFiner resolution between 0.25 and 0.3 would narrow the bound but would not change the qualitative finding or the practical recommendation to set $r$ above 0.3.\nA level-based trigger keyed to baseline SLV could detect the transient dip in Type~II trajectories, but would not activate under Type~III where SLV exceeds baseline at all generations.\n",

```text
1	
2	\paragraph{Model Collapse.}
3	\citet{shumailov2024collapse} showed that recursive training on model-generated data progressively erodes the tails of learned distributions, a phenomenon they termed \emph{model collapse}.
4	\citet{dohmatob2024tails} formalized this decay through scaling laws that predict tail erosion as a function of recursive depth, and \citet{dohmatob2024demystified} provided a regression-theoretic analysis of the underlying mechanism.
5	The self-consuming loop accelerates collapse even under data accumulation~\citep{alemohammad2024mad}, and \citet{dohmatob2025strong} demonstrated that as little as 1\% synthetic contamination suffices to trigger degradation, though \citet{gerstgrasser2024inevitable} showed that preserving all prior real data can delay it.
6	These studies diagnose collapse through output distributions, benchmark scores, or theoretical scaling laws.
7	We complement them by tracking collapse through the spectral geometry of internal representations, which requires no text generation or external evaluation.
8	Unlike neural collapse~\citep{papyan2020prevalence}, which describes last-layer features converging to a simplex ETF in classification, spectral collapse in recursive training reflects progressive embedding volume loss unrelated to class structure.
9	
10	\paragraph{Spectral Analysis of Representations.}
11	Random matrix theory (RMT) applied to neural network weights reveals implicit regularization structure in the eigenspectrum~\citep{martin2021implicit}.
12	Effective rank~\citep{roy2007effectiverank} and its normalized variant RankMe~\citep{rankme2023} have become standard tools for quantifying representational dimensionality.
13	Recent work has connected spectral properties to training pathologies: \citet{he2025spectral} linked spectral collapse in hidden representations to plasticity loss in continual learning, and \citet{naderi2025mindgap} identified spectral rank collapse in attention matrices as a failure mode of deep transformers.
14	Most directly relevant, \citet{gu2026sigma} proposed SIGMA, a spectral monitoring framework that tracks singular-value distributions during recursive training on OPT-125M over three generations.
15	Our work extends SIGMA along several axes.
16	In \emph{scale}, we track Pythia-410M and pilot Pythia-1B, 3--8$\times$ larger than OPT-125M.
17	In \emph{temporal depth}, we follow spectral evolution across 10 recursive generations, revealing a three-phase trajectory (decompression--onset--accelerating collapse) that is not visible at the 3-generation horizon of prior work.
18	At three generations, spectral indicators are still rising (Figure~\ref{fig:spectral_trajectory}), which could lead to the premature conclusion that spectral health implies model health.
19	In \emph{methodology}, we move from open-loop observation to closed-loop control: our SLV-based controller actively adjusts the real-data mixing ratio during training (\S\ref{subsec:controller}).
20	In \emph{metric choice}, we adopt the Spectral Log-Volume (SLV, the log-determinant of the embedding Gram matrix) rather than effective rank or spectral norm, capturing joint changes across the full singular-value spectrum.
21	Finally, the \emph{signal attenuation paradox}, whereby successful mixing suppresses the very spectral indicators that would have warned of collapse, is a phenomenon that short-horizon, open-loop monitoring does not encounter.
22	That SIGMA observed analogous spectral shifts on OPT-125M (a different architecture family) provides preliminary cross-architecture evidence for the generality of spectral collapse dynamics.
23	
24	\paragraph{Early Warning Signals and Critical Transitions.}
25	The theory of early warning signals (EWS) for critical transitions originates in ecology, where \citet{scheffer2009ews} established that rising autocorrelation and variance precede regime shifts in complex systems.
26	\citet{scheffer2012anticipating} broadened this framework by identifying architectural features (such as network modularity and feedback structure) that predispose complex systems to tipping points, and argued that combining model-based and observation-based indicators improves anticipation of critical transitions.
27	\citet{dakos2012methods} developed a methodological toolbox for detecting these signals, and \citet{bury2021deeplearning} showed that deep learning can identify EWS in systems where classical indicators fail.
28	A central tension in EWS theory is that stabilization interventions suppress the very precursors they rely on~\citep{scheffer2009ews, scheffer2012anticipating}.
29	We encounter a parallel tension in model collapse, but the failure mode is distinct: ecological stabilization reduces the signal-to-noise ratio while preserving signal direction, whereas real-data mixing reverses the spectral trajectory and eliminates the signal entirely (\S\ref{subsec:controller}).
30	To our knowledge, this \emph{signal attenuation paradox}, in which the intervention that prevents collapse simultaneously destroys the spectral indicator that would have predicted it, has no direct analogue in the ecological EWS literature and represents a qualitatively distinct failure mode not previously characterized in the EWS literature.
31	\citet{he2025spectral} provides a bridge between these domains by connecting spectral collapse to critical transitions in continual learning.
32	
33	\paragraph{Data Mixing for LLM Training.}
34	A growing body of work studies how training data composition shapes language model quality.
35	\citet{longpre2024pretrainer} systematically varied data age, domain coverage, quality, and toxicity across 28 1.5B-parameter models, finding that heterogeneous sources consistently improve downstream performance but that temporal distribution shift degrades it.
36	\citet{muennighoff2023scaling} extended Chinchilla scaling laws to the data-constrained regime, showing that up to four epochs of data repetition incur negligible loss degradation but that returns diminish sharply beyond this threshold.
37	On the optimization side, DoReMi~\citep{xie2023doremi} optimizes domain weights through a small proxy model, Data Mixing Laws~\citep{ye2025mixinglaws, data_mixing_laws2024} derive predictive formulas for optimal composition from small-scale experiments, and Aioli~\citep{chen2025aioli} unifies several online mixing strategies under a single framework.
38	These approaches uniformly evaluate mixing through loss or downstream benchmark scores.
39	Our work reveals a complementary dimension: the mixing ratio between real and synthetic data reshapes the spectral geometry of internal representations, producing a phase diagram (\S\ref{subsec:phase_diagram_exp}) that loss-based metrics alone do not capture.
40	Our controller further differs in targeting a specific pathology, recursive spectral collapse, through a spectral signal (SLV) rather than optimizing general domain balance.
41

_source line 409 · 2026-05-21T04:21:00.365Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01AhKpWKvnPZKsrZZAHZWYkR`</summary>

```text
1	
2	We presented a systematic spectral characterization of model collapse across 10 recursive generations of Pythia-410M and identified a three-phase trajectory (decompression, onset, acceleration) confirmed at the 1B scale through the onset phase.
3	SLV is particularly suited for automated monitoring: it requires only a forward pass, declines monotonically after its peak, and captures volume changes before they manifest as dimensionality reduction.
4	By varying the mixing ratio across five levels, we construct a collapse phase diagram that localizes $r_{\text{crit}} \in (0.25, 0.3]$: above this threshold SLV expands monotonically (Type~III), while just below it a transitional regime (Type~II) exhibits an initial contraction transient followed by recovery.
5	A proof-of-concept proportional controller exposes a signal attenuation paradox.
6	The maximum per-generation controller signal weakens monotonically with the real-data fraction: $\max \Delta_t = 0.0019$ at $r{=}0.2$ (just below $\tau{=}0.002$), $0.00028$ at $r{=}0.25$ ($6.7\times$ weaker), and no decline signal at $r \geq 0.3$ where SLV expands monotonically.
7	The controller falls silent along a continuous gradient, not at a single threshold.
8	Because all seven EWS indicators reverse under sufficient mixing (\S\ref{subsec:alt_ews}), this paradox generalizes beyond SLV: any per-generation reactive controller faces the same tradeoff between prevention and detectability~\citep{scheffer2009ews}.
9	Even when mixing prevents spectral collapse, monitoring retains value: $r_{\text{crit}}$ varies across domains and architectures, and spectral indicators reveal whether the current ratio lies in the transitional or stable zone without waiting for downstream degradation.
10	
11	Although reactive controllers fail under signal attenuation, $r_{\text{crit}}$ provides a practical alternative.
12	Calibrating the critical threshold once for a given architecture and domain allows setting $r > r_{\text{crit}}$ as a static mixing floor, reducing the problem from real-time detection to one-time calibration and sidestepping the detection--prevention tradeoff entirely.
13	We note that $r_{\text{crit}}$ marks the boundary for \emph{spectral} collapse prevention; output diversity (as measured by D-4) does not monotonically improve with $r$ and may decrease at high mixing ratios due to anchoring to the fixed real-data pool (Table~\ref{tab:phase_summary}).
14	Spectral collapse prevention ensures that the embedding space retains sufficient geometric diversity to support downstream adaptation; quality degradation at fixed $r$ is linear and predictable (Table~\ref{tab:phase_summary}), unlike the exponential acceleration observed under spectral collapse.
15	Choosing an operational mixing ratio therefore involves a tradeoff between spectral stability and output diversity.
16	For practitioners, this reduces collapse prevention to a single offline calibration: sweeping a few mixing ratios with forward-pass spectral checks to locate $r_{\text{crit}}$, then fixing $r$ above this floor permanently, requiring no per-generation text generation, benchmark evaluation, or monitoring infrastructure during recursive training.
17	
18	The signal attenuation paradox also extends the classical EWS framework~\citep{scheffer2009ews} in a qualitative direction.
19	In ecological critical transitions, warning signals weaken through declining signal-to-noise ratio but retain their directional relationship to the approaching threshold; in model collapse, the intervention reverses the spectral trajectory itself, so the signal vanishes through trajectory inversion rather than noise, a failure mode not previously characterized in the EWS literature.
20	
21	As web corpora become increasingly saturated with model-generated content, the spectral monitoring and calibration framework proposed here extends naturally to this implicit-loop setting, where the mixing ratio is not controlled but can be estimated post hoc.
22	
23	The signal attenuation paradox motivates four directions for future work.
24	First, cumulative indicators that integrate spectral trajectory shape across generations may detect sub-critical regimes where per-generation differences vanish.
25	Second, non-spectral auxiliary signals (perplexity acceleration, diversity collapse rate) could provide complementary triggers that do not attenuate with mixing.
26	Third, hybrid approaches combining spectral EWS with periodic probing at reduced real-data fractions may sustain controller sensitivity without sacrificing collapse prevention.
27	Fourth, under data accumulation~\citep{gerstgrasser2024inevitable}, the effective real-data fraction grows with each generation; we conjecture that the signal attenuation paradox applies analogously, as any sufficiently large real-data share would suppress the spectral contraction signal. Empirical verification remains future work.
28	Validation at larger scales and longer horizons remains essential.
29	
```

Now I need to do a thorough cross-section consistency check and structure audit. Let me also read the paper-quality-lessons skill as required by the audit checklist §7.

Let me systematically go through the consistency checks:

## Part 1: Post-edit consistency check

### A. Abstract claims vs Introduction C1-C4

**Abstract L7**: "localizes the critical mixing ratio $r_{\text{crit}}$ to the interval $(0.25, 0.3]$ across two random seeds"
**Intro C4 (L29)**: "empirical localization of $r_{\text{crit}}$ robust to the EMA smoothing parameter and random seed"


The Abstract is more specific about the number of seeds while the Intro frames it more broadly as robustness — both are consistent, just at different levels of detail.

**Abstract L5**: "reproducible three-phase pattern: spectral decompression (generations 0--2), where effective rank and spectral log-volume (SLV) increase even as downstream performance degrades; collapse onset (generations 2--4), where spectral indicators reverse; and accelerating collapse (generations 4--10), where spectral contraction accelerates."
**Intro C1 (L26)**: "The first multi-seed spectral-downstream characterization at 410M+ scale, establishing a reproducible three-phase collapse pattern (decompression, onset, acceleration) across 10 generations"

These align well — the Intro captures the same three-phase structure at the stated scale.

**Abstract L6**: "SLV is particularly suited for automated monitoring: it requires only a forward pass on held-out data, captures distributed volume changes across all spectral directions, and exhibits post-decompression monotonic decline amenable to threshold-based control."
**Intro C2 (L27-28)**: "SLV as a computationally cheap collapse monitor with monotonic post-peak decline and a HellaSwag-spectral asymmetry: downstream degradation begins during the spectral decompression phase, demonstrating that spectral expansion does not indicate model health"

There's a slight gap here — the Abstract emphasizes threshold-based control as a practical advantage, while the Intro focuses on the HellaSwag-spectral asymmetry and the key insight that spectral expansion doesn't guarantee downstream health. The Intro adds a concrete empirical finding that the Abstract doesn't explicitly state.

**Abstract L8**: "signal attenuation paradox: moderate real-data mixing prevents the spectral contraction that would activate the controller, so reactive spectral feedback becomes ineffective precisely when prevention succeeds."
**Intro C3 (L28)**: "A signal attenuation paradox: moderate real-data mixing suppresses the spectral signal reactive controllers depend on, a structural limitation formalized via $r_{\text{crit}}$"

These are aligned — both identify the same paradox and its mechanism.

Now checking the Conclusion against the Experiments section. The Conclusion claims the three-phase trajectory holds through the onset phase at 1B scale, and the Experiments section appears to confirm this with Pythia-1B across generations 0-3.

For the SLV monitoring claim, the Conclusion emphasizes that SLV captures volume changes before dimensionality reduction manifests, but I'm seeing that both SLV and r_eff peak at the same generation in the pure collapse case. Since SLV measures log-volume while r_eff measures entropy of singular values, I need to verify whether one actually precedes the other or if they're just measuring different aspects of the same phenomenon.

Looking at the mixing regime data, at r=0.2 the smoothed SLV shows contraction while r_eff continues rising, which does support the claim that SLV detects the transition earlier. So Conclusion L3 holds up.

Now for Conclusion L4, I'm checking the critical threshold where the behavior shifts from Type II (initial contraction then recovery) to Type III (monotonic expansion), and the experiments are narrowing down that boundary to somewhere between 0.25 and 0.3.

For Conclusion L6, I'm tracking how the maximum per-generation controller signal decays as we increase the real-data fraction—it's 0.0019 at r=0.2 (below the threshold), drops to 0.00028 at r=0.25 (about 6.7 times weaker), and disappears entirely once r≥0.3 where SLV expands monotonically.

L7 captures the key insight: this isn't a sharp phase transition but a smooth gradient where the controller gradually loses its ability to influence the system.

For the new content, L9 makes a forward-looking argument that even when mixing prevents spectral collapse, monitoring r_crit remains valuable because it likely varies across different domains and architectures—though this goes beyond what the experiments directly test. L13 appears to be discussing output diversity metrics, but the thought cuts off there. Looking at the actual data more carefully — the claim compares linear degradation at r above the critical threshold versus exponential degradation at r=0, but Table 3 only shows endpoint values at generation 10, not the trajectory itself. The r=0 case showing PPL jump from 9.55 to 73.64 is clearly exponential, but whether r=0.5's increase from 9.55 to 15.10 qualifies as "linear" would require examining the per-generation progression to verify.

The paper also repeats nearly identical language about the signal attenuation paradox being a novel failure mode in both the Related Work and Conclusion sections, which is consistent but somewhat redundant. Additionally, the final claim about extending the framework to implicit-loop settings with model-generated content isn't directly tested in the experiments, so it's more of a forward-looking suggestion than a validated finding.

Now looking at the method section, the controller uses a clipping function that adjusts the mixing ratio based on remaining budget and a threshold for detecting divergence, with specific hyperparameter values of 0.002 for the threshold and 10 for the sensitivity scaling.

Checking the experimental results against these parameters, I see the adaptive controller stayed inactive across all generations since the maximum divergence signal was consistently below the threshold—the largest value at generation 2 was 0.0019, just under the 0.002 cutoff. This confirms the clipping bounds and the overall mechanism are working as intended.

Moving to related work, I'm comparing how SIGMA's model sizes relate to the Pythia models we're tracking.  The 3-8× scaling claim is accurate: Pythia-410M is 3.3 times larger than OPT-125M, and Pythia-1B is 8 times larger. The earlier "order of magnitude" framing was an overstatement.

I'm also verifying that the ten-generation horizon reveals something genuinely new—the three-phase spectral trajectory only becomes apparent over that longer window, whereas prior work at three generations would have missed the inflection point and potentially misinterpreted rising spectral indicators as a sign of model health. The shift from passive observation to active control through the closed-loop mechanism aligns with what we describe in the methods section.

The cross-architecture evidence from OPT-125M is appropriately hedged as preliminary, which matches our limitations discussion about whether this pattern generalizes. The signal attenuation paradox we identify is a novel finding without precedent in ecological early warning systems literature.

Now I'm checking for em-dashes throughout the paper to ensure consistency in formatting. Now I'm verifying the key numerical values across tables — Table 1 shows Gen 10 means of r_eff=440.62, SLV=2194.86, and PPL=73.64, which match Table 3's r=0 row when accounting for rounding, and the abstract's mention of the (0.25, 0.3] range aligns with the experimental setup. I've spotted one potential issue where a conclusion about volume changes is supported by only a single data point at r=0.2.

The claim about linear quality degradation at fixed r is problematic — the paper only shows endpoints at Gen 10 for r≥0.3 without demonstrating the intermediate trajectory, so calling it "linear" is overstating what the data actually supports. There's also some redundancy between the conclusion and related work sections where they use nearly identical language about this being an uncharacterized failure mode. The experiments section mentions seed variance at r=0.25 but the thought cuts off there. I need to reconcile the inconsistency between Table 3's single-seed claim and line 142's two-seed EMA comparison. The issue is that line 142 references seed43 data from exp_014, which only goes through generation 2, while the paper presents r=0.25 as having complete single-seed coverage through generation 10. I should clarify that line 142 is specifically discussing early boundary sensitivity with partial data, not implying full trajectory coverage for the second seed.

Looking at the limitations section, it correctly states that r=0.25 reports a single seed for the full trajectory—the early EMA data from seed43 doesn't constitute a complete replication, so the statement is accurate as written.

For the timing claims in the experiments section, there's an unresolved issue: the benchmark script exists but hasn't been run, so these numbers remain unverified. The <1 second claim for SLV computation seems reasonable given the small matrix size, but the ~70 minute HellaSwag evaluation time needs actual verification before publication.

Now examining the paper's structure, there's no dedicated Discussion section—it flows from Introduction through Methods, Experiments, Related Work, Conclusion, and Limitations. The Conclusion section is doing double duty as both discussion and conclusion.

Looking at how the Conclusion is organized, it moves from summarizing the three-phase trajectory and SLV's monitoring value, into the phase diagram and signal attenuation paradox, then pivots to r_crit as a practical alternative with a calibration workflow for practitioners. From there it extends to the EWS framework, touches on web contamination, and ends with four future directions plus a validation caveat. The flow from practical workaround back to theoretical extensions feels a bit disjointed—I should check whether the transitions between these ideas are clear enough.

When exp_013 and exp_014 complete, I'll need to revisit the r_crit bounds in P2, since tightening the range from (0.25, 0.3] depends on whether r=0.25 data comes through. The 1B scale confirmation in P1 won't change. The rest of the Conclusion should hold as-is.

For the Experiments section, I'm tracking which tables and captions need updates: Table 1 and Table 3 will need cross-seed averaging once the three-seed runs finish, and several inline references to "two seeds" will shift to "three seeds." The r=0.25 trajectory data in the later paragraphs will also need the new cross-seed means incorporated.

Now I'm checking the Limitations section to see what seed count language needs adjusting there, and I want to systematically go through for any em-dash formatting issues I might have missed.

_source line 422 · 2026-05-21T04:23:35.575Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The `---` matches are all in table cells (used as "N/A" or "no data" markers) — these are em-dashes in LaTeX tables, standard practice for "not applicable". Not problematic.

Now let me verify the "linear and predictable" claim. Table 3 shows Gen 10 endpoints, but the claim is about quality degradation trajectory over generations at fixed r. The paper doesn't show intermediate PPL values for r=0.3 or r=0.5 across generations. Let me check if there's per-generation data for these conditions on the server.

Actually, from the paper's perspective, the claim "quality degradation at fixed $r$ is linear and predictable" refers to PPL increasing with generations at a fixed r above r_crit. 

But Table 3 only provides final-generation snapshots, so it can't actually support a claim about linearity across generations. Meanwhile, at r=0 the PPL trajectory is 9.55 → 13.57 → 24.60 → 38.17 → 73.64, which looks exponential rather than linear. The paper only shows two data points at r=0.5 without intermediate values, so I can't verify whether the growth is actually linear there either. This seems like an unsupported claim that I should flag.

Now I'm checking whether the Conclusion's claim about capturing volume changes before dimensionality reduction manifests is actually supported. The evidence cited shows that at r=0.2, EMA-smoothed SLV declines while r_eff still rises, but under pure collapse at r=0 both peak simultaneously at Generation 2. So this "before" relationship only holds in certain mixing regimes, not as a universal principle.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HCsYfKEzhyLGVV4DR7BNqL`</summary>

````text
1	---
2	name: paper-quality-lessons
3	description: NeurIPS 投稿实战经验总结——人工审查中提炼的写作规范、审查清单、常见反模式。Paper Agent / Reviewer / Director 写论文或审查论文时加载。
4	allowed-tools: [Read, Write, Edit, Glob, Grep]
5	scope: global
6	version: 1
7	tags:
8	  - writing
9	  - paper
10	  - quality
11	  - checklist
12	  - review
13	roles:
14	  - agent
15	  - reviewer
16	  - director
17	  - paper_agent
18	---
19	
20	# 论文写作质量指南（NeurIPS 实战经验）
21	
22	> 来源：2026 年 NeurIPS 投稿期间，从 20+ 项目的人工审查反馈中提炼。
23	> 每条规则都源自真实的 bug / 退回 / 审稿质疑。
24	
25	---
26	
27	## 一、数据诚信与溯源（最高优先级）
28	
29	### 1.1 数字必须有源头
30	
31	- 论文中**每一个数字**必须可追溯到 registry 实验 ID 或原始结果文件
32	- 禁止凭记忆写数字——必须 `cat result.json | head` + `ls -la` 附上文件证据
33	- 计算值（均值、标准差、DID、delta）必须独立重算，不信 Agent 第一手汇报
34	- `paper_provenance.yaml` 覆盖率必须 100%，改数字必须同步更新 provenance
35	
36	> **教训（D149 事件）**：Agent 曾伪造完整的 metrics 数据包括 p-value，事后被 Reviewer 独立 parse JSON 抓出。从此所有关键数据必须 Reviewer 独立验证到字段级别。
37	
38	### 1.2 不同来源数据不能混用
39	
40	- 配对数据必须同 seed、同 c 值、同 N、同 evaluation protocol
41	- 两张表如果 protocol 不同（如 0-shot vs 8-shot），论文必须明确说明
42	- 跨 config 比较 delta 会引入系统误差——必须补跑同 config baseline 后才能比较
43	- 非等量对比必须显式标注（如 "at unmatched volumes"）
44	
45	> **教训（D027 事件）**：不同 config 文件的 baseline 不同，跨 config 比较 delta 值引入了 artifact。
46	
47	### 1.3 结论撤回后不可复活
48	
49	- 被 Director shelved 的数据（如因截断混淆），禁止重新引入论文
50	- `paper_provenance.yaml` 中 `verified: false` 的 claim，不得恢复
51	- 术语修正后不可回退（如 "same norm" → "same per-layer standard deviation"）
52	
53	---
54	
55	## 二、Claim 强度与数据匹配
56	
57	### 2.1 Claim 强度规则
58	
59	| 数据支撑 | 允许的最强表述 |
60	|----------|--------------|
61	| 单 seed, 单数据集 | "preliminary observation", "initial evidence" |
62	| 单 seed, 多数据集 | "suggestive pattern", "indicative trend" |
63	| 多 seed, 单数据集 | 报告均值±标准差，可写 "we find" |
64	| 多 seed, 多数据集 | 可用 "robust", "consistent" |
65	| 统计检验显著 | 可用 "significant", 附 p-value |
66	| 单 seed + 外推到其他条件 | 必须加限定，不能声称 "scale-invariant" 等 |
67	
68	### 2.2 禁止的过强表述
69	
70	- "remarkably stable" → 改为 "relatively stable" 或给出量化波动范围
71	- "confirmed" → 仅 "suggestive" 级别数据不能用
72	- "scale-invariant" → single-seed 验证不够
73	- "We attribute X to Y" → 如果是假说，必须改为 "We hypothesize" + "requiring causal verification"
74	- "roughly twice" → 实际比值必须在 1.8x-2.2x 才能用，否则改为更准确的近似
75	- "domain-agnostic" → 改为 "robust across N domains tested"
76	
77	### 2.3 Claim 降级机制
78	
79	当证据不足以支撑独立 claim 时，应降级为 discussion 中的定性讨论，而非删除。具体做法：
80	- 独立 contribution → Discussion 段落
81	- 硬 claim → 加 caveat 的软 claim
82	- 正文数字 → 移入 Appendix
83	
84	---
85	
86	## 三、叙事与 Storytelling
87	
88	### 3.1 结构原则
89	
90	- 核心 paradox/发现之间必须有**过渡句**桥接，不能让读者在关键处断线
91	- Introduction 每条 Contribution 必须有对应的 section/table 支撑
92	- Discussion 不应重复 Results 的具体数字——引用表格编号
93	- 同一解释不要在多处重复推导——主文写一次 + 指向 Appendix
94	- Limitations 段不要写成一个 17 行大段——拆分为 2-3 个子段按主题分组
95	
96	### 3.2 冷引用（Cold Reference）
97	
98	- 任何首次出现的概念必须有上文铺垫
99	- Introduction 贡献列表中不能突然出现未被提及的模型/方法名
100	
101	### 3.3 不要用旧叙事套新数字
102	
103	新 baseline 出来后若 delta 大幅变化，Section 的叙事逻辑必须重写，不是简单替换数字。
104	
105	### 3.4 Contingency Plan
106	
107	关键实验结果不确定时需预写两套叙事（如 "若结果 A → 叙事 A"、"若结果 B → 叙事 B"），实验完成后选择正确版本。提交前必须确认只留了一套。
108	
109	### 3.5 不要基于中间值做叙事决策
110	
111	70% 进度时的估计往往不够稳定（尤其 GSM8K 等难度分布不均匀的 benchmark），必须等完整数据再定稿叙事。
112	
113	---
114	
115	## 四、术语与一致性
116	
117	### 4.1 术语一致性
118	
119	- 全文必须统一使用正式定义的术语，不得出现同义词漂移
120	- 术语定义后，每次使用必须完全一致（不要有时 "perturbation sensitivity" 有时 "perturbation impact"）
121	
122	### 4.2 统计量一致性
123	
124	- 两个不同的 kappa/p-value 每次出现时必须标注是哪个
125	- 同一个 metric 在不同位置的数字必须完全一致
126	- 修改任何数字后必须全文 grep 确认所有引用处同步
127	
128	### 4.3 p-value 格式
129	
130	- 无前导零、3 位小数：`p = .003`（不是 `p = 0.003`）
131	- 当非劣效性 p<0.001 实际由正向效应驱动时，必须加括注说明
132	
133	### 4.4 例子与定义必须一致
134	
135	Taxonomy 中的定义和对应示例不能自相矛盾。如果定义说 "no new content"，示例中就不能包含新信息。
136	
137	---
138	
139	## 五、投稿前检查清单（Pre-Submission Checklist）
140	
141	### 5.1 占位符扫描（硬门禁）
142	
143	提交前必须 grep 扫描，以下模式要求**零匹配**：
144	```
145	XX.XX, PLACEHOLDER, [If , TODO, TBD, \placeholder{
146	```
147	所有条件占位段落（`[If ...]`）必须替换为最终文本。
148	
149	### 5.2 编译检查
150	
151	- [ ] 编译零 error
152	- [ ] 无 `??` 未解析引用
153	- [ ] 无 > 1pt 的 overfull hbox
154	- [ ] 正文 ≤ 9 页（NeurIPS desk reject 风险）
155	- [ ] 使用当年 style file（`neurips_2026.sty`）
156	- [ ] Review 版启用行号
157	- [ ] 匿名审稿版无作者姓名、无致谢
158	
159	### 5.3 数据完整性
160	
161	- [ ] 论文所有数字与 registry/provenance 交叉验证通过
162	- [ ] 计算值（均值、标准差、DID、delta）独立重算确认
163	- [ ] 所有 evaluation protocol 差异已在论文中说明
164	- [ ] 多 seed 结果报告均值和标准差
165	- [ ] 单 seed 数据在 Limitations 中声明
166	
167	### 5.4 图表检查
168	
169	- [ ] 所有 figure PDF 存在且路径匹配
170	- [ ] 图表不超栏宽（单栏 5.5in / 全宽 6.5in）
171	- [ ] 图中字体 ≥ 7pt
172	- [ ] 小数精度全表统一
173	- [ ] 最优结果加粗、次优下划线
174	- [ ] 表格标题（caption）自包含
175	- [ ] Forest plot 等可视化无 dummy 值（CI_LO=-99 等）
176	
177	### 5.5 引用检查
178	
179	- [ ] 引用覆盖：**直接相关方法、baseline 论文、评测 benchmark 论文、理论基础**（任一缺失说明调研不充分）
180	- [ ] 引用密度与目标会议近年录用论文同量级
181	- [ ] 引用键与 bib 条目 1:1 匹配
182	- [ ] 无孤儿 bib 条目
183	- [ ] 无幻觉引用（所有被引论文实际存在）
184	- [ ] 近年论文的年份/会议准确
185	- [ ] 审稿人要求的引用全部补齐
186	
187	### 5.6 匿名化检查
188	
189	- [ ] 源代码无硬编码绝对路径（`/Users/...`, `/root/...`）
190	- [ ] 配置文件无服务器路径
191	- [ ] Git history 已清理 author 信息（用 fresh repo）
192	- [ ] 项目管理文件不得提交（AGENTS.md, memory.md, registry.yaml, task_context/ 等）
193	- [ ] API Keys 通过环境变量，无泄露
194	
195	### 5.7 会议 Checklist 填写（如目标会议要求）
196	
197	- [ ] Claims: 摘要/Introduction 准确反映实验范围
198	- [ ] Limitations: 正文 Limitations 段 + Appendix 补充
199	- [ ] Experiments: 实验设置完整（exp_id, seed, 超参, 评估 protocol）
200	- [ ] Reproducibility: 数据集公开、代码可用
201	- [ ] Statistical Significance: 多 seed 均值+标准差、置信区间
202	- [ ] Compute: GPU 型号、训练时间、总 GPU-hours
203	- [ ] Licenses: 数据集/模型引用完整
204	- [ ] AI Assistants: 如实声明使用情况
205	- [ ] 每项认真回答（不要全写 N/A）
206	
207	### 5.8 叙事完整性
208	
209	- [ ] Conclusion 包含所有关键限定符（如 "under greedy decoding"）
210	- [ ] Abstract 与正文一致（不能一个写 "single seed" 另一个已是 "10 seeds"）
211	- [ ] 无 Appendix Version A/B 两套叙事残留
212	- [ ] 无 LaTeX 注释与正文混淆
213	- [ ] Contribution claim 在 Introduction 中（不在 Related Work 中）
214	- [ ] 所有专有名词/缩写首次出现时有定义（Abstract 内缩写也必须展开）
215	- [ ] Introduction 与 Method/Results 讲同一条主线（idea 变化后全文逻辑一致）
216	
217	### 5.9 去 AI 风格检查
218	
219	- [ ] **`grep "—" paper/*.tex` 全文清零**（解释性 em-dash 必须全删，最显著的 AI 痕迹）
220	- [ ] **`grep " – " paper/*.tex` 全文清零**（解释性 en-dash 必须全删）
221	- [ ] 无 "-ing," 分词短语结尾（如 "..., demonstrating the effectiveness of..."）
222	- [ ] 无连续 "Furthermore" / "Moreover" / "Additionally"（同段最多 1 个）
223	- [ ] 无 "It is worth noting that..." / "Notably, ..."
224	- [ ] 无 "delve into" / "leverage" / "utilize" / "a myriad of" / "a plethora of"
225	- [ ] 自然段 5-8 句是正常的，不要每 2-3 句就换段
226	
227	### 5.10 模板与同类论文核对
228	
229	- [ ] LaTeX 模板从目标会议官网下载（不是他人转载、不是上一年）
230	- [ ] `\documentclass[]{}` 选项匹配 review / camera-ready 阶段
231	- [ ] 写作前已参考 2-3 篇同领域近 1-2 年优秀论文，叙事节奏对齐
232	
233	---
234	
235	## 六、Reviewer 审查标准
236	
237	### 6.1 CRITICAL 级别（必须修复才能提交）
238	
239	- 论文数字与实验数据不一致
240	- Abstract 与正文矛盾
241	- Evaluation protocol 混淆（如 0-shot 写成 8-shot）
242	- 未经验证的数据写入论文
243	- 占位符残留
244	
245	### 6.2 WARNING 级别（应当修复）
246	
247	- Stale claims（更新了某处数字但忘了同步其他位置）
248	- 过强措辞不匹配数据支撑
249	- 隐式全因子设计误导（如写 "3×3×3=27" 但实际只有 15 格）
250	- 评估指标只描述了部分 domain
251	- 旧版数据引用
252	
253	### 6.3 数据一致性审查流程
254	
255	1. 从 registry 提取所有实验 metrics
256	2. 对照论文中每一个内联数字（到行号级别）
257	3. 独立重算所有衍生值
258	4. 不一致处标注为 CRITICAL
259	5. 近似表述检查实际比值是否在允许范围内
260	
261	---
262	
263	## 七、常见反模式速查表
264	
265	| 反模式 | 症状 | 修复方式 |
266	|--------|------|----------|
267	| Stale claim | 论文某处还引用旧数字 | 改数字后全文 grep 同步 |
268	| Protocol 混淆 | 不同表格用了不同 evaluation 但没说明 | 每张表注明 protocol |
269	| Agent 误报 | 实验结果缺乏文件证据 | 要求 `cat result.json` 截图 |
270	| 混用数据算比值 | 跨 seed/条件/N 的数据混合比较 | 只用严格配对的数据 |
271	| 叙事超越数据 | "confirmed" 但实际只是 suggestive | 匹配 claim 强度到 §2.1 |
272	| 冷引用 | 首次出现的概念无铺垫 | 先建立框架再引入 |
273	| 定义-例子矛盾 | Taxonomy 定义和示例冲突 | 审查所有定义与其示例 |
274	| Placeholder 残留 | XX.XX, TODO, [If...] 未清理 | 提交前 grep 硬门禁 |
275	| Path 泄露 | 代码/config 含服务器绝对路径 | 改为相对路径 |
276	| 重复推导 | 同一解释在 3 处各自推导一遍 | 主文一次 + 引用 Appendix |
277	| Discussion 重复 Results | Discussion 大量复制 Results 数字 | 改为引用表格编号 |
278	| 单 seed overclaim | 单 seed 数据声称 scale-invariant | 加 preliminary/hedging 限定 |
279	| Registry 不同步 | Registry 面值与实际状态不一致 | 以 SSH 实测为准 |
280	| vLLM 静默失败 | max_model_len 太小导致 100% 400 错误 | 预检必须发真实 prompt 验证 |
281	
282	---
283	
284	## 八、写论文前加载的 Skills
285	
286	写论文或做最终审查时，Director/Agent 应按需加载以下 skills：
287	- `latex-paper` — LaTeX 编译与格式
288	- `reference-audit` — 引用真实性审计
289	- `academic-writer` — ML 学术写作规范
290	- `paper-quality-lessons`（本 skill）— 实战经验教训
291	
292	---
293	
294	## 九、附录：投稿经验时间线教训
295	
296	1. **论文数字锁定时机**：最终编译前 24h 锁定所有数字，之后只允许格式修改
297	2. **GPU 不要空闲**：审稿期间如果 Agent 空闲，应该跑 ablation/扩展实验作为 rebuttal 储备
298	3. **匿名化提前做**：不要等到最后一天才检查路径泄露，fresh repo 需要时间清理
299	4. **9 页零余量策略**：NeurIPS 正文 9 页是硬限，每次新增内容必须等量压缩并编译验证
300	5. **Contingency 叙事提前写**：关键实验完成前就写好两版叙事，避免最后一刻仓促改稿
301	
````

</details>

Grep]\nscope: global\nversion: 1\ntags:\n  - writing\n  - paper\n  - quality\n  - checklist\n  - review\nroles:\n  - agent\n  - reviewer\n  - director\n  - paper_agent\n---\n\n# 论文写作质量指南（NeurIPS 实战经验）\n\n> 来源：2026 年 NeurIPS 投稿期间，从 20+ 项目的人工审查反馈中提炼。\n> 每条规则都源自真实的 bug / 退回 / 审稿质疑。\n\n---\n\n## 一、数据诚信与溯源（最高优先级）\n\n### 1.1 数字必须有源头\n\n- 论文中**每一个数字**必须可追溯到 registry 实验 ID 或原始结果文件\n- 禁止凭记忆写数字——必须 `cat result.json | head` + `ls -la` 附上文件证据\n- 计算值（均值、标准差、DID、delta）必须独立重算，不信 Agent 第一手汇报\n- `paper_provenance.yaml` 覆盖率必须 100%，改数字必须同步更新 provenance\n\n> **教训（D149 事件）**：Agent 曾伪造完整的 metrics 数据包括 p-value，事后被 Reviewer 独立 parse JSON 抓出。从此所有关键数据必须 Reviewer 独立验证到字段级别。\n\n### 1.2 不同来源数据不能混用\n\n- 配对数据必须同 seed、同 c 值、同 N、同 evaluation protocol\n- 两张表如果 protocol 不同（如 0-shot vs 8-shot），论文必须明确说明\n- 跨 config 比较 delta 会引入系统误差——必须补跑同 config baseline 后才能比较\n- 非等量对比必须显式标注（如 \"at unmatched volumes\"）\n\n> **教训（D027 事件）**：不同 config 文件的 baseline 不同，跨 config 比较 delta 值引入了 artifact。\n\n### 1.3 结论撤回后不可复活\n\n- 被 Director shelved 的数据（如因截断混淆），禁止重新引入论文\n- `paper_provenance.yaml` 中 `verified: false` 的 claim，不得恢复\n- 术语修正后不可回退（如 \"same norm\" → \"same per-layer standard deviation\"）\n\n---\n\n## 二、Claim 强度与数据匹配\n\n### 2.1 Claim 强度规则\n\n| 数据支撑 | 允许的最强表述 |\n|----------|--------------|\n| 单 seed, 单数据集 | \"preliminary observation\", \"initial evidence\" |\n| 单 seed, 多数据集 | \"suggestive pattern\", \"indicative trend\" |\n| 多 seed, 单数据集 | 报告均值±标准差，可写 \"we find\" |\n| 多 seed, 多数据集 | 可用 \"robust\", \"consistent\" |\n| 统计检验显著 | 可用 \"significant\", 附 p-value |\n| 单 seed + 外推到其他条件 | 必须加限定，不能声称 \"scale-invariant\" 等 |\n\n### 2.2 禁止的过强表述\n\n- \"remarkably stable\" → 改为 \"relatively stable\" 或给出量化波动范围\n- \"confirmed\" → 仅 \"suggestive\" 级别数据不能用\n- \"scale-invariant\" → single-seed 验证不够\n- \"We attribute X to Y\" → 如果是假说，必须改为 \"We hypothesize\" + \"requiring causal verification\"\n- \"roughly twice\" → 实际比值必须在 1.8x-2.2x 才能用，否则改为更准确的近似\n- \"domain-agnostic\" → 改为 \"robust across N domains tested\"\n\n### 2.3 Claim 降级机制\n\n当证据不足以支撑独立 claim 时，应降级为 discussion 中的定性讨论，而非删除。具体做法：\n- 独立 contribution → Discussion 段落\n- 硬 claim → 加 caveat 的软 claim\n- 正文数字 → 移入 Appendix\n\n---\n\n## 三、叙事与
